\displaystyle H(x)=e^{-(1+{\gamma}_{E})x^{2}}G(1+ix)G(1-ix)\,.

(5.105)

The function
Z_{\rm inst}(m,a,\tau)
depends nonperturbatively on
g_{\rm YM}
via
e^{-{8\pi^{2}\over g_{\rm YM}^{2}}}
and contains the instanton contributions (the anti-instanton contributions are captured by
\overline{Z_{\rm inst}(m,a,\tau)}
)
[
118
,
119
]
. For
m=0
, this becomes the familiar Gaussian Hermitian matrix model
[
79
,
120
]
. One important property of
Z_{\text{1-loop}}
and
Z_{\rm inst}
is that they are even functions of
m
and satisfy

\left.\partial_{m}Z_{\text{1-loop}}\right|_{m=0}=\left.\partial_{m}Z_{{\rm inst}}\right|_{m=0}=0\,.

(5.106)

Since the integrated insertions are naturally associated to derivatives of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
preserving parameters
\tau,\bar{\tau}
and
m
in the SYM action on
{\rm S}^{4}
, we expect that the integrated four-point function can be computed from

\displaystyle\left.{\partial_{\tau}^{J\over 2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}^{J^{\prime}\over 2}\partial_{m}^{2}Z(\tau,m)\over Z(\tau,m)}\right|_{m=0}

(5.107)

for even non-negative integers
J,J^{\prime}
. Importantly here we do not restrict
J=J^{\prime}
. The reason is to take into account operator mixing on
{\rm S}^{4}
, which arises owing to curvature and mass deformation (see e.g.
[
28
]
),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{J}=\mathcal{O}_{J}^{\prime}+\sum_{K=1}^{J\over 2}u_{J,J-2K}{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{J-2K}\,.

(5.108)

Here
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{J}
is the natural basis of operators on
{\rm S}^{4}
that are generated by the action of
\partial_{\tau}^{J/2}
or
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}^{J/2}
, and corresponds to an insertion
a^{J}
in the integral (
5.103
). On the other hand,
{\mathcal{O}}_{J}
is the basis of operators on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
whose two-point functions are orthogonal,
\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{J},{\mathcal{O}}_{J^{\prime}}\rangle\propto\delta_{J,J^{\prime}}
.
Thus using a polynomial

Q_{J}(a)=a^{J}+\sum_{K=1}^{\frac{J}{2}}u_{J,J-2K}a^{J-2K}\,.

(5.109)

we can compute
G_{J}
by the following matrix integrals

G_{J}=\left.\frac{\partial_{m}^{2}\left(\int d\tilde{\mu}(m)\,Q_{J}(a)Q_{J}(a)\right)}{\int d\tilde{\mu}(m)\,Q_{J}(a)Q_{J}(a)}\right|_{m=0}

(5.110)

where the denominator gives the two-point function of
\mathcal{O}_{J}
’s and is needed to make
\mathcal{O}_{J}
unit-normalized. The mass-deformed measure
d\tilde{\mu}(m)
is given by

\displaystyle d\tilde{\mu}(m)\equiv da\,(2a^{2})e^{-4\pi{\rm Im}\tau a^{2}}Z_{\text{1-loop}}(m,a,\tau)|Z_{\rm inst}(m,a,\tau)|^{2}\,.

(5.111)

Thanks to the orthogonality of the two-point functions (in the absence of mass deformation),
Q_{J}
’s satisfy the condition,

\displaystyle\int d\mu\,Q_{J}(a)Q_{J^{\prime}}(a)\propto\delta_{J,J^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\qquad d\mu\equiv d\tilde{\mu}(0)=da\,(2a^{2})e^{-4\pi{\rm Im}\tau a^{2}}\,,

(5.112)

which uniquely fixes the coefficients
u_{J,J-2K}
as functions of
\tau
and
\bar{\tau}
. Applying the Gram-Schmidt procedure following
[
28
,
33
]
, we can write down
Q_{J}
explicitly as follows:

\displaystyle Q_{J}(a)=\frac{1}{D_{J}}\left|\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathcal{I}_{0}&\mathcal{I}_{2}&\cdots&\mathcal{I}_{J}\\
\mathcal{I}_{2}&\mathcal{I}_{4}&\cdots&\mathcal{I}_{J+2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathcal{I}_{J-2}&\mathcal{I}_{J}&\cdots&\mathcal{I}_{2J-2}\\
1&a^{2}&\cdots&a^{2J}\end{array}\right|\,,

\displaystyle D_{J}=\det\left(\mathcal{I}_{2(j+k-2)}\right)_{1\leq j,k\leq\frac{J}{2}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{I}_{j}\equiv\int d\mu\,a^{j}\,.

(5.118)

Two important identities that follow from the Gram-Schmidt procedure is the expression for the normalization of the two-point function,

\int d\mu\,Q_{J}(a)Q_{J^{\prime}}(a)=\frac{D_{J+2}}{D_{J}}\delta_{J,J^{\prime}}\,,

(5.119)

and an “emergent” matrix-model representation for
D_{J}

[
33
,
23
]
,

D_{J}=\frac{1}{(J/2)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{\frac{J}{2}}\underbrace{\left(dx_{i}\sqrt{x_{i}}e^{-4\pi{\rm Im}\tau x_{i}}\right)}_{d\mu}\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\,.

(5.120)

As shown above, the factor in the middle is the measure
d\mu
written in terms of the variable
x=a^{2}
.

Matrix model for integrated correlators.

We now derive the matrix model representation for the integrated correlator (
5.110
) following the approach in
[
37
]
. We first consider orthogonal polynomials under the mass-deformed measure
d\tilde{\mu}(m)
,

\displaystyle\int d\tilde{\mu}(m)\,\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\tilde{Q}_{J^{\prime}}(a)=\frac{\tilde{D}_{J+2}}{\tilde{D}_{J}}\delta_{J,J^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)=a^{J}+\sum_{K=1}^{\frac{J}{2}}\tilde{u}_{J,J-2K}a^{J-2K}\,,

(5.121)

where
\tilde{u}_{J,K}
now depends both on
\tau
and
m
, and
\tilde{D}_{J}
admits a matrix-model type representation

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{J}=\frac{1}{(J/2)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{\frac{J}{2}}\left(dx_{i}\sqrt{x_{i}}e^{\mathcal{F}(\sqrt{x_{i}})}e^{-4\pi{\rm Im}\tau x_{i}}\right)\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\,,

(5.122)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(a)\equiv\log Z_{\text{1-loop}}(m,a,\tau)+\log|Z_{\rm inst}(m,a,\tau)|^{2}\,.

Taking the second derivative of (
5.121
) with respect to
m
and setting
m=0
, we obtain the term that gives
G_{J}
(
5.110
) and two additional terms:

\displaystyle\left.\partial_{m}^{2}\left(\int d\tilde{\mu}(m)\,\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\right)\right|_{m=0}=

(5.123)

\displaystyle\left.\partial_{m}^{2}\left(\int d\tilde{\mu}(m)\,Q_{J}(a)Q_{J}(a)\right)\right|_{m=0}+\left.2\int d\mu\left(\partial_{m}\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\right)^{2}\right|_{m=0}+\left.2\int d\mu\,Q_{J}(a)\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\right|_{m=0}\,.

However, since
Z_{\text{1-loop}}
and
Z_{\rm inst}
are even functions of
m
(see (
5.106
)), the second term in (
5.123
) vanishes. Furthermore, since
\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)
is a polynomial of degree less than
J
, the last term also vanishes owing to the orthogonality (
5.119
). We thus obtain
45
45

45

In the second equality, we used the fact that the localization integrand is an even function of
m
, (
5.106
).
the following simple expression for
G_{J}
:

G_{J}=\left.\frac{\partial_{m}^{2}\left(\int d\tilde{\mu}(m)\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\right)}{\int d\tilde{\mu}(m)\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)\tilde{Q}_{J}(a)}\right|_{m=0}=\left.\left(\frac{\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{D}_{J+2}}{\tilde{D}_{J+2}}-\frac{\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{D}_{J}}{\tilde{D}_{J}}\right)\right|_{m=0}\,.

(5.124)

Using (
5.122
), we arrive at the formula that expresses the integrated correlator as an expectation value of a “single-trace operator” in the matrix model

G_{J}=\left<{\rm tr}\left[f\left(M\right)\right]\right>_{J+2}-\left<{\rm tr}\left[f\left(M\right)\right]\right>_{J}\,,

(5.125)

where the function
f(a)
is given by

f(x)\equiv\left.\partial_{m}^{2}\mathcal{F}(\sqrt{x})\right|_{m=0}=\left.\partial_{m}^{2}\left(\log Z_{\text{1-loop}}(m,\sqrt{x},\tau)+\log|Z_{\rm inst}(m,\sqrt{x},\tau)|^{2}\right)\right|_{m=0}\,,

(5.126)

and
\left<\bullet\right>_{J}
is the expectation value in the Wishart-Laguerre matrix model of size
J/2
,

\left<{\rm tr}\left[f\left(M\right)\right]\right>_{J}=\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{\frac{J}{2}}\left(dx_{i}\sqrt{x_{i}}e^{-4\pi{\rm Im}\tau x_{i}}\right)\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{J}{2}}f(x_{k})}{\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{\frac{J}{2}}\left(dx_{i}\sqrt{x_{i}}e^{-4\pi{\rm Im}\tau x_{i}}\right)\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}\,.

(5.127)

The formula (
5.125
) is valid at finite
\tau
and
J
, but it simplifies in the large charge ’t Hooft limit as the limit coincides with the standard ’t Hooft limit of this matrix model.
In this case, the (anti)instanton contributions are non-perturbatively suppressed in
J
and we can set
|Z_{\rm inst}|=1
.
Then, using the following integral formula
46
46

46

This can be derived by using the following two formulae:

\displaystyle\log H(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n+1}\zeta(2n+1)x^{2n+2}\,,\qquad\zeta(2n+1)=\frac{2^{2n}}{\Gamma(2n+2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\frac{w^{2n+1}}{\sinh^{2}(w)}\,.

given in (A.12) of
[
121
]
,

\log H(x)=\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{dw}{w}\frac{1-2w^{2}x^{2}-\cos(2wx)}{2\sinh^{2}(w)}\,,

(5.128)

we can rewrite (
5.126
) with

\displaystyle f(x)=8\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\frac{\sin^{2}(2\sqrt{x}w)}{\sinh^{2}(w)}\,.

(5.129)

To the leading large
J
limit, the matrix model is
solved by the distribution
[
23
]
,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(y)={1\over 2\pi}\sqrt{4-y\over y}\,,\quad y\in[0,4]\,,

(5.130)

where
y
is related to the eigenvalue
x
by
x=\frac{\lambda_{J}y}{16\pi^{2}}
.
Consequently, (
5.125
) can be evaluated in the large
J
limit by

\displaystyle G_{J}=\int_{0}^{4}dy\rho_{0}(y)(\lambda_{J}\partial_{\lambda_{J}}+1)f\left(\frac{\lambda_{J}y}{16\pi^{2}}\right)\,.

(5.131)

Starting from the expression (
5.129
) for
f(a)
and integrating over
x
first, using

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{4}dy\,\rho_{0}(y)\sin^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{J}y}{4\pi^{2}}}w\right)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\pi J_{1}\left(\frac{2w\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}}{\pi}\right)}{2w\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}}\,,

(5.132)

we obtain

\displaystyle G_{J}=4\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\frac{w}{\sinh^{2}(w)}\left(1-J_{0}\left(\frac{2w\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}}{\pi}\right)\right)\,.

(5.133)

It is straightforward to obtain the weak coupling (small
\lambda_{J}
) expansion from (
5.133
) by expanding the Bessel function and performing the integral. The result reads

G_{J}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}2^{2-2n}(2n+1)!\zeta(2n+1)}{\pi^{2n}(n!)^{2}}\lambda_{J}^{n}\,\,.

(5.134)

This agrees
47
47

47

There is a difference of an overall factor of
4
as compared to the result in
[
54
]
owing to the difference in the normalization of operators. See e.g. (3.29) in
[
54
]
.
with the results obtained in recent papers
[
54
]
and
[
55
]
, which used different methods.
48
48

48

Note that the definitions of the large charge ’t Hooft coupling in
[
54
,
55
]
are slightly different:
\lambda_{J}^{\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Paul:2023rka,Brown:2023why}{}{}]}}}=g_{\rm YM}^{2}J
.

It is easy to see that the weak coupling expansion has a finite radius of convergence
|\lambda_{J}|<\pi^{2}
.
This coincides with the radius of convergence for the structure constant of the Konishi operator (
5.29
). We will discuss their common physical origin in Section
5.5.2
.

Comparison with the direct large charge computation.

The result (
5.134
) can also be reproduced by a direct integration of the four-point function at large charge, which we computed in Section
5.3
.

The first step is to note the following relation between the HHLL four-point function
t(z,\bar{z})
in (
5.8
) and the un-integrated four-point function
{\mathcal{T}}_{J}(U,V)
in
(
5.102
),

\mathcal{T}_{J}(U,V)=\frac{4}{z\bar{z}}\left(t(z,\bar{z})^{2}-1\right)\,.

(5.135)

The prefactor
1/z\bar{z}
comes from
\Theta(U,V,Y_{ij})
(see
[
105
]
) and the overall numerical factor
4
is to account for the correct normalization. The subtraction
-1
is needed to remove the tree-level contribution. We next use the crossing symmetry under
z,\bar{z}\to 1/z,1/\bar{z}
and restrict the range of integration of
r
to
[0,1]
:

G_{J}=-\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}dr\int_{0}^{\pi}d\varphi\left.{r^{3}\sin^{2}\varphi\over U^{2}}{\mathcal{T}}_{J}(U,V)\right|_{U=1+r^{2}-2r\cos\varphi,V=r^{2}}\,.

(5.136)

We then express
t(z,\bar{z})
as a sum over magnons (
5.9
) and perform the integral explicitly. The result reads

G_{J}=4\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{a}-\frac{a^{2}}{(a^{2}+16g^{2})^{3/2}}\right)\,.

(5.137)

It is then straightforward to check that the weak-coupling expansion of (
5.137
) is in precise agreement with the result from the matrix model (
5.134
).

A trick to evaluate the integral at strong coupling.

To obtain the strong coupling (large
\lambda_{J}
) expansion, we evaluate the remaining
w
integral by contour deformation. A similar trick gives a proof of the conjectured formula (4.21) in
[
23
]
.

We first rewrite the integral as a contour integral over
{\gamma}
just above the real line defined by

\displaystyle{\gamma}=[-\infty,-\epsilon]\cup C_{\epsilon}\cup[\epsilon,\infty]\,,

(5.138)

where
C_{\epsilon}
is an infinitesimal semicircle of radius
\epsilon
centered at the origin in the upper half-plane. Using the relation between the Bessel function
J_{0}(x)
and the Hankel function
H_{0}^{(1)}(x)
,

\displaystyle H^{(1)}_{0}(x)-H^{(1)}_{0}(-x)=2J_{0}(x)\,,

(5.139)

we have

\displaystyle 2\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}dw{w\over\sinh^{2}w}J_{0}\left(2w\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}\over\pi\right)=\left(\int_{-\infty}^{-\epsilon}dw+\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}dw\right){w\over\sinh^{2}w}H_{0}^{(1)}\left(2w\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}\over\pi\right)\,.

(5.140)

Furthermore, the Hankel function
H_{0}^{(1)}(x)
has a branch point at
x=0
and behaves at small
x
as

\displaystyle H_{0}^{(1)}(x)=1+{2i\over\pi}\left(\log{x\over 2}+\gamma_{E}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}(x)\,.

(5.141)

Consequently

\displaystyle 2\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}dw\,\frac{w}{\sinh^{2}(w)}+\int_{C_{\epsilon}}dw{w\over\sinh^{2}w}H_{0}^{(1)}\left(2w\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}\over\pi\right)=2(1+\gamma_{\rm E})+\log{\lambda_{J}\over 4\pi^{2}}\,.

(5.142)

Putting together (
5.140
) and (
5.142
), we obtain

\displaystyle G_{J}=2\log{\lambda_{J}\over 4\pi^{2}}+4(1+\gamma_{\rm E})-2\int_{\gamma}dw{w\over\sinh^{2}(w)}H_{0}^{(1)}\left(2w\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}\over\pi\right)\,.

(5.143)

Deforming the contour upwards, one picks up residues at
w=n\pi i
for positive integer
n
. From the relation between Hankel function on the positive imaginary axis and Bessel functions,

\displaystyle H_{t}^{(1)}(ix)={2\over\pi i^{t+1}}K_{t}(x)\,,

(5.144)

and the recurrent relations for Bessel functions, we thus obtain the following explicit expression for the integrated four-point function

\displaystyle G_{J}=2\log{\lambda_{J}\over 4\pi^{2}}+4(1+{\gamma}_{E})+8\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(2n\sqrt{\lambda_{J}}K_{1}(2n\sqrt{\lambda_{J}})-K_{0}(2n\sqrt{\lambda_{J}})\right)\,.

(5.145)

Here again, the Bessel functions on the RHS are exponentially suppressed for large
\lambda_{J}
and correspond to the worldline instanton contributions to the integrated correlator, as is expected from the form of the un-integrated four-point function
t(z,\bar{z})
given in (
5.14
).

Using the
following Mellin integral expression for the Bessel functions
K_{t}(2y)
with
t>0
,

\displaystyle K_{t}(2y)={1\over 8\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}ds\,y^{-s}\Gamma\left({s+t\over 2}\right)\Gamma\left({s-t\over 2}\right)\,,

(5.146)

where
\epsilon>t
and
y=n\sqrt{\lambda_{J}/2}
,
we can equivalently write (
5.145
) in the following way,

\displaystyle G_{J}=2\log{\lambda_{J}\over 4\pi^{2}}+4(1+{\gamma}_{E})+\int_{0^{+}-i\infty}^{0^{+}+i\infty}{ds\over 2\pi i}\lambda^{-{s\over 2}}{2(s-1)}\zeta(s)\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)^{2}\,.

(5.147)

Note that the weak coupling expansion (
5.134
) immediately follows from (
5.147
) by deforming the contour to the left and picking up the residues.

5.5.2
Radius of convergence and massless magnon point

One interesting outcome of our analysis is that both the three-point function of the Konishi operator and the integrated four-point function have the same (finite) radius of convergence
|\lambda_{J}|=\pi^{2}
and they become singular at
\lambda_{J}=-\pi^{2}
. Precisely at this point, the mass of the lightest massive magnon,

\sqrt{1+16g^{2}}=\sqrt{1+\frac{\lambda_{J}}{\pi^{2}}}\,,

(5.148)

vanishes. It is therefore natural to conjecture that the physical origin of the singularity is a tachyonic instability induced by this magnon.

A few comments are in order. First, this relation between singularity and the emergence of massless particles is reminiscent of the structure of the Coulomb branch moduli space in
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theories, which also exhibits singularity when BPS particles become massless
[
122
,
123
]
. In fact, using the dictionary between the large
R
-charge and the Coulomb branch moduli (
5.65
), one can map the massless magnon point at large charge to the massless W-boson point.

Second, in perturbation around a trivial vacuum
\langle\phi\rangle_{\rm cl}=0
, the theory becomes unstable as soon as the coupling
g_{\rm YM}^{2}
becomes negative and the perturbation series has a zero radius of convergence. What we found implies that the large charge ’t Hooft vacuum is more stable than the trivial vacuum owing to the background
R
-charges that delays the onset of the instability. One interesting question for the future is to take other quantum numbers (in particular the Lorentz spin) to be large and study the stability.

Third, the radius of convergence is finite also in the standard large
N_{c}
’t Hooft limit. This often allows one to take the
double-scaled large-
N
limit
in which one zooms in the singularity. The limit corresponds to the continuum limit of Feynman graphs and the critical behavior near the singularity is governed by (gravitationally dressed) conformal field theory
[
124
,
125
,
126
]
. It would be interesting to take a similar double-scaled limit around
\lambda_{J}=-\pi^{2}
and study it from the point of view of Feynman diagrams. Another interesting question is to find an effective (conformal) field theory
49
49

49

A natural guess is a theory that contains both the Goldstone boson around the large charge vacuum and the magnon that becomes massless at the singularity.
governing the critical behavior around
\lambda_{J}=-\pi^{2}
.

5.5.3
Generalization to
{\rm SU}(N)
gauge theories

We now generalize the result to
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM with the SU
(N)
gauge group. In
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFTs with higher-rank gauge groups, there are multiple chiral operators with the identical
R
-charge, and in general they all mix with each other when the theory is placed on
{\rm S}^{4}
. This makes the Gram-Schmidt procedure and the derivation of the large-charge matrix model significantly more complicated. However, in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, it was shown in
[
28
]
that operators organize into a set of “towers” and the mixing occurs only within each tower. This is a special property of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. See Section 3.2.1 of
[
28
]
for more details.

To define the towers of operators, we consider the set of operators made out of single-trace operators of length greater than 2, i.e.
\prod_{k=3}^{N}\left(\mathcal{S}_{k}\right)^{n_{k}}
and order them such that their charges are non-decreasing;
B_{n}
with
J_{n}\leq J_{n+1}
. We then construct an orthogonal basis
\mathcal{O}_{0}^{(n)}
by the following recursive procedure,

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n)}\equiv B_{n}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n^{\prime}<n\\
J_{n}-J_{n^{\prime}}\in 2\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left<B_{n}\,,\,\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n^{\prime})}\right>}{\left<\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\frac{J_{n}-J_{n^{\prime}}}{2}}^{(n^{\prime})}\,,\,\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n^{\prime})}\right>}\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\frac{J_{n}-J_{n^{\prime}}}{2}}^{(n^{\prime})}\,,

(5.149)

with

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{(n)}\equiv\left(\mathcal{S}_{2}\right)^{k}\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n)}\,.

(5.150)

For the first few
n
,
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n)}
takes the following form:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(0)}=1\,,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(1)}=\mathcal{S}_{3}\,,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(2)}=\mathcal{S}_{4}-\frac{\langle\mathcal{S}_{4}\rangle}{\langle(\mathcal{S}_{2})^{2}\rangle}(\mathcal{S}_{2})^{2}\,.

(5.151)

Unlike the Gram-Schmidt procedure we used for the SU
(2)
gauge theory, the operators
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n)}
’s only contain
B_{n}
’s with the same charge. Consequently, there is still a residual mixing among operators with different charges. A special property of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM is that the residual mixing occurs only among operators in the same tower,
\{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1}^{(n)},\cdots\}
. Within each tower, there is only a single operator with a given
R
-charge and therefore the mixing can be resolved in the same way as in the
{\rm SU}(2)
theory;

\mathcal{O}_{k}^{(n)}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{(n)}+\sum_{K=1}^{k}u_{k,k-K}^{(n)}\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{k-K}^{(n)}\,.

(5.152)

General formula.

The
{\rm S}^{4}
partition function of the mass-deformed
{\rm SU}(N)
SYM is
[
79
]
,

Z(\tau,m)={1\over N!}\int\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}da_{i}\right)\left(\prod_{i<j}(a_{i}-a_{j})^{2}\right)\delta({\rm tr}({\text{\boldmath$a$}}))e^{-2\pi{\rm Im}\tau{\rm tr}({\text{\boldmath$a$}}^{2})}Z_{\text{1-loop}}(m,{\text{\boldmath$a$}},\tau)\left|Z_{\text{inst}}(m,{\text{\boldmath$a$}},\tau)\right|^{2}\,,

(5.153)

with
{\text{\boldmath$a$}}={\rm diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})
and

\displaystyle Z_{\text{1-loop}}(m,{\text{\boldmath$a$}},\tau)={1\over H(m)^{N-1}}\prod_{i\neq j}\frac{H(a_{i}-a_{j})}{H(a_{i}-a_{j}+m)}\,.

(5.154)

The operators
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{(n)}
and
\mathcal{O}_{k}^{(n)}
correspond to the following insertions in the localization integral

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{(n)}\,\,\mapsto\,\,g^{(n)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\left({\rm tr}\left({\text{\boldmath$a$}}^{2}\right)\right)^{k}\,,\qquad\mathcal{O}_{k}^{(n)}\,\,\mapsto\,\,g^{(n)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})Q_{k}^{(n)}\left({\rm tr}\left({\text{\boldmath$a$}}^{2}\right)\right)\,,

(5.155)

where
g^{(n)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})
is an insertion corresponding to the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{0}^{(n)}
, whose form can be deduced from its structure (
5.151
), and
Q_{k}^{(n)}(x)
is a polynomial of degree
k
defined by
50
50

50

Note that the normalization of
x
is slightly different from the one we used in the
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge theory. In terms of the eigenvalue
a
for the
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge theory,
x
here is given by
2a^{2}
while
x
in the previous analysis was given by
a^{2}
.

Q_{k}^{(n)}(x)=x^{k}+\sum_{K=1}^{k}u_{k,k-K}^{(n)}x^{k-K}\,.

(5.156)

We then follow
[
36
]
and separate the integral into the integral along the “radial” coordinate
x={\rm tr}({\text{\boldmath$a$}}^{2})
and the integral of “angular” coordinates
\int_{S^{N-1}}d\Omega
with the traceless constraint,

\int\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}da_{i}\right)\left(\prod_{i<j}(a_{i}-a_{j})^{2}\right)\delta({\rm tr}({\text{\boldmath$a$}}))=\int_{0}^{\infty}dx\,x^{\frac{N^{2}-3}{2}}\int_{S^{N-1}}d\Omega\,J(\Omega)\,,

(5.157)

where
J(\Omega)
is a Jacobian for the change of coordinates. For later convenience we define the constant
C_{N}=\int d\Omega\,J(\Omega)
and the normalized angular integration

\displaystyle\llangle f({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rrangle=\frac{1}{C_{N}}\int_{S^{N-1}}d\Omega J(\Omega)f({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\,,

(5.158)

which produces a function of the radial variable
x={\rm tr}({\text{\boldmath$a$}}^{2})
. By construction, the angular integration is closely related to the full matrix integral for the hermitian Gaussian matrix model,

\displaystyle\langle f({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rangle_{\rm GMM}\equiv{1\over{\mathcal{N}}}\int\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}da_{i}\right)\left(\prod_{i<j}(a_{i}-a_{j})^{2}\right)f({\text{\boldmath$a$}}){\delta}(\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits{\text{\boldmath$a$}})e^{-\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits({\text{\boldmath$a$}}^{2})}\,,

(5.159)

where
{\mathcal{N}}
is a normalization factor such that
\langle 1\rangle_{\rm GMM}=1
. For example if
f
is homogeneous of degree
\Delta

[
36
]
,

\displaystyle\llangle f({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rrangle={x^{\frac{\Delta}{2}}\Gamma({N^{2}-1\over 2})\over\Gamma({N^{2}-1+\Delta\over 2})}\langle f({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rangle_{\rm GMM}\,,

(5.160)

which will be useful for us later.

The polynomials
Q_{k}^{(n)}
are orthogonal to each other under the radial measure
d\mu^{(n)}

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int d\mu^{(n)}Q_{k}^{(n)}(x)Q_{k^{\prime}}^{(n)}(x)=\frac{D^{(n)}_{k+1}}{D^{(n)}_{k}}\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}\,,\quad d\mu^{(n)}\equiv dx\,x^{\frac{N^{2}-3}{2}}e^{-2\pi{\rm Im}\tau x}\llangle\left(g^{(n)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\right)^{2}\rrangle\,,

(5.161)

where
D^{(n)}_{k}
admits a ‘matrix model’ representation

D^{(n)}_{k}=\frac{1}{k!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}d\mu^{(n)}(x_{i})\right)\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\,.

(5.162)

To derive a matrix model for the integrated correlators, we consider the mass-deformed measure
d\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\equiv dx\,x^{\frac{N^{2}-3}{2}}e^{-2\pi{\rm Im}\tau x}\llangle\left(g^{(n)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\right)^{2}e^{{\mathcal{F}}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})}\rrangle

where
\mathcal{F}=\log Z_{\text{1-loop}}+\log|Z_{\rm inst}|^{2}
.
Following the derivation for the
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge theory, we arrive at the following representation for the integrated correlator of the operators
\mathcal{O}_{k}^{(n)}

G_{k}^{(n)}=\left<{\rm tr}\left[f^{(n)}\left(M\right)\right]\right>_{k+1}-\left<{\rm tr}\left[f^{(n)}\left(M\right)\right]\right>_{k}\,,

(5.163)

where
\langle\bullet\rangle_{k}
is the expectation value in the following matrix model of size
k
:

\left<{\rm tr}\left[f^{(n)}\left(M\right)\right]\right>_{k}=\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(d\mu^{(n)}(x_{i})\right)\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k}f^{(n)}(x_{j})}{\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(d\mu^{(n)}(x_{i})\right)\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}\,,

(5.164)

and the function
f^{(n)}(x)
is defined by the angular integral

f^{(n)}(x)\equiv\frac{\llangle\left(g^{(n)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\right)^{2}\partial_{m}^{2}\left.\mathcal{F}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\right|_{m=0}\rrangle}{\llangle\left(g^{(n)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\right)^{2}\rrangle}\,,

(5.165)

As in the case of the
{\rm SU}(2)
theory, the formula (
5.163
) is valid at finite
\tau
,
N
and
J
.

Result for maximal-trace operators.

The matrix integral (
5.164
) takes a particularly simple form for
\mathcal{O}_{k}^{(0)}
’s, which are often called the “maximal-trace” operators. In this case
g^{(0)}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})=1
and the radial measure (
5.161
) coincides with the measure for the Wishart-Laguerre (WL) matrix model

d\mu^{(0)}=dx\,x^{\frac{N^{2}-3}{2}}e^{-2\pi{\rm Im}\tau x}\,.

(5.166)

Note that, even for
n=0
, the observable
f^{(n)}(x)
takes a rather complicated form since it is defined by an angular integral (
5.165
).

We
use the integral representation of
\log H(x)
(
5.128
) and obtain

\displaystyle\left.\partial_{m}^{2}\mathcal{F}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\right|_{m=0}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\frac{w{\cal I}(w{\text{\boldmath$a$}})}{\sinh^{2}(w)}\,,\quad{\cal I}({\text{\boldmath$a$}})\equiv 8\sum_{i<j}\sin^{2}(a_{i}-a_{j})\,.

(5.167)

The first step is to evaluate the angular integral of
{\cal I}(wa)
, which we can write by expanding in
w
as,

\displaystyle\llangle{\cal I}(w{\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rrangle=\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}x^{s}w^{2s}b(s,N)\,.

(5.168)

We will come back to the explicit form of
b(s,N)
shortly. Let us proceed by performing the remaining rank
k
WL matrix integral and the
w
integral in (
5.167
). The latter is straightforward with the following identity

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dw{w^{2s+1}\over\sinh^{2}(w)}=2^{-2s}\Gamma(2s+2)\zeta(2s+1)\,.

(5.169)

The WL matrix integral with measure (
5.166
) is different from the
N=2
case considered in the previous section. However, to the leading order in the large
k
limit, the eigenvalue distribution is
N
independent (see e.g.
[
23
]
Appendix A) and given by
(
5.130
). The relation between the
y
variable in (
5.130
) and the WL eigenvalue
x
is
x={\lambda_{J}y\over 8\pi^{2}}
here, where a factor 2 originates from the different normalization conventions for general
N
as explained in footnote
50
.
From the integral identity

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{4}dy\rho_{0}(y)y^{s}={1\over s+1}{(2s)!\over(s!)^{2}}\,,

(5.170)

together with (
5.169
),
we then obtain the following formula for the integrated correlator of the maximal trace operators with charge
J
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{J/2}^{(0)}
,

\displaystyle G_{J/2}^{(0)}=\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}2^{-s}\pi^{-1-2s}{(2s+1)}\zeta(2s+1)\Gamma\left(s+{1\over 2}\right)^{2}b(s,N)\lambda_{J}^{s}\,.

(5.171)

We now come back to the explicit form of
b(s,N)
.
As a consequence of (
5.160
), the coefficients
b(s,N)
are related to those from the Gaussian matrix model integral,

\displaystyle\langle{\cal I}(w{\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rangle_{\rm GMM}=\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}2^{s}w^{2s}\tilde{b}(s,N)\,,\quad b(s,N)=2^{s}\tilde{b}(s,N){\Gamma({N^{2}-1\over 2})\over\Gamma({N^{2}-1\over 2}+s)}\,.

(5.172)

The integral
\langle{\cal I}(w{\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rangle_{\rm GMM}
has been studied previously (see for example

[
127
,
112
]
) and it determines completely the integrated correlator of the stress energy tensor multiplet in the
{\rm SU}(N)
SYM
[
109
,
110
,
111
]
. In particular, the coefficient
\tilde{b}(s,N)
admits a meromorphic extension to complex
s
, captures the spectral decomposition of the
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
invariant correlator into non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
and obey a three-term recursion relation in
N
. Here we see that via (
5.172
), the same quantity completely determines the integrated correlator of large charge maximal-trace operators.

By now there are several equivalent expressions for
\langle{\cal I}(w{\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rangle_{\rm GMM}
which can be derived by standard resolvent methods. Here we use the following expression which can be deduced from formulae in Appendix A
[
112
]
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}(t)=e^{-t}L_{N-1}^{(1)}(t)^{2}+N\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}e^{-t^{\prime}}\left(L_{N-1}^{(1)}(t^{\prime})L_{N-1}(t^{\prime})-L_{N-2}^{(1)}(t^{\prime})L_{N}(t^{\prime})\right)-N^{2}\,,

(5.173)

where
L_{n}^{({\alpha})}(t)
are the generalized Laguerre polynomials and

\langle{\cal I}(w{\text{\boldmath$a$}})\rangle_{\rm GMM}=-2{\mathcal{K}}(2w^{2})
. The constant is fixed by
{\mathcal{K}}(0)=0
.

The Mellin transform of
{\mathcal{K}}(t)
easily follows from the orthogonality of the Laguerre polynomials and integration by part as in
[
112
]
,
51
51

51

This is related to the function
c(s,N)
in
[
112
]
by an overall factor of
{1\over 4}s(s-1)(1-2s)
.
The function
c(s,N)
determines the integrated correlator of the stress tensor multiplet in
[
105
]
completely as shown in
[
109
,
110
,
111
]
.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,{t^{s-1}\over\Gamma(s)}{\mathcal{K}}(t)=\,{N(N-1)(1-2s)\,}{}_{3}F_{2}(2-N,1-s,s;2,3;1)\,.

(5.174)

On the other hand, the coefficients
\tilde{b}(s,N)
in (
5.172
) are picked out by the residue at origin,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}(s,N)=-2\oint{dt\over 2\pi i}t^{-s-1}{\mathcal{K}}(t)\,.

(5.175)

Nonetheless, by considering a contour deformation in (
5.174
) with the new contour
{\gamma}^{\prime}
that comes in from
\infty
just below the positive real axis, circles the origin by a small semicircle
C^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
and goes back to the infty just above the positive real axis,

\displaystyle{\gamma}^{\prime}=[-i\epsilon+\infty,-i\epsilon]\cup C^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\cup[i\epsilon,i\epsilon+\infty]\,,

(5.176)

it is easy to obtain the following relation

\displaystyle\tilde{b}(s,N)={-2e^{-\pi is}\over\Gamma(s+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt{t^{-s-1}\over\Gamma(-s)}{\mathcal{K}}(t)\,,

(5.177)

and consequently from (
5.174
),

\displaystyle\tilde{b}(s,N)={-2(-1)^{s}(1+2s)\over\Gamma(s+1)}N(N-1){}_{3}F_{2}(2-N,1+s,-s\,;\,2,3\,;\,1)\,.

(5.178)

We thus arrive at the final formula by combining (
5.172
) and (
5.178
),

\displaystyle G_{J/2}^{(0)}=

\displaystyle-2N(N-1)\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}{(-1)^{s}((2s+1)!)^{2}\over(s!)^{3}}\zeta(2s+1)\left(\frac{\lambda_{J}}{16\pi^{2}}\right)^{s}

(5.179)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\times\,}_{3}F_{2}(2-N,-s,s+1;2,3;1){\Gamma({N^{2}-1\over 2})\over\Gamma({N^{2}-1\over 2}+s)}\,,

which reduces to (
5.134
) for
N=2
and agrees with the result in the recent paper
[

]
derived from recursion relations in both
N
and

[
113
]
(up to a factor of 4 as noted in Footnote
47
).
The radius of convergence in (
5.179
) is
N
independent and coincide with (
5.29
).

We expect the matrix model approach described here to be useful to study more general large charge integrated correlators that involve operators
\mathcal{O}_{k}^{(n)}
that are not of the maximal trace type (i.e.
n\neq 0
).
It would also be interesting to analyze
the non-planar contributions in the large charge limit,
as well as
other scaling limits such as the large
N
-large
J
limit
52
52

52

In the limit
J\propto N^{2}
with fixed

,
x^{\frac{N^{2}-3}{2}}
in (

) becomes dominant and the integral (

) can be approximated by a matrix model with a logarithmic potential.

J\propto N^{2}
discussed in
[
54
]
. We leave the more extensive analysis to future work.

6
Conclusions and future directions

The results of this paper highlight the large charge ’t Hooft limit as a new and intriguing solvable corner of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM that shares striking similarities with the conventional large
N_{c}
limit. Through the analysis of the spectrum and correlation functions, we demonstrated the validity of this claim. However, we emphasize that our analysis only scratched the surface of this interesting limit, and there are countless exciting directions for future exploration.

6.1
Direct generalization of the results in this paper

Index and partition function at large charge.

In Section
2.4
, we showed using the contour deformation tricks that the superconformal index and the partition function simplify in the large charge limit and can be rewritten as a sum over magnons. The analysis was done only for the
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge group and for the large charge limit of
1/2
BPS operators. It would be interesting to generalize it to higher-rank gauge groups and to less supersymmetric states. If a similar rewriting exists for
1/16
BPS states, it could be relevant for the microstate counting of supersymmetric black holes, about which significant progress has been made recently
[
128
,
129
,
130
]
.

Constraints on CFT data from dilaton Ward identity.

In Section
5.4.3
, we conjectured the implication of the dilaton Ward identity on the CFT data. However the argument presented there was rather heuristic and it is important to make them rigorous and prove them from the conformal bootstrap. This will be the first step towards understanding necessary and sufficient conditions for CFTs to have the vacuum moduli.

Integrated correlator at large charge.

In Section
5.5
, we derived an emergent matrix model description for the integrated correlator both for the
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge group and general
{\rm SU}(N)
gauge groups. However, for the
{\rm SU}(N)
gauge groups, we only analyzed the simplest class of large charge operators (i.e. the maximal trace operators) and it would be interesting to perform explicit computations for other large charge operators. In addition, we expect that the matrix-model reformulation to be useful for other limits such as the combined large
N
-large
J
limit (
J\propto N^{2}
) discussed in
[
54
]
.

Massless magnon point, double-scaling limit, and EFT.

One interesting outcome of our analyses is that various quantities in the large charge ’t Hooft limit become singular at
\lambda_{J}=-\pi^{2}
. As discussed in Section
5.5
, this singularity signals a tachyonic instability of the lightest magnon. It would be interesting to see if one can define a “double-scaling limit” near this instability, much like the double-scaling limit of matrix integrals studied in the past
[
124
,
125
,
126
]
. Based on the analogy with the matrix integrals, we expect that this limit is dominated by “dense” Feynman diagrams at large charge. It would be interesting to identify diagrams that dominate in the limit and come up with a “continuum” description. A related question is to understand the effective (conformal) field theory describing such a limit.

Exact worldline instanton.

In (
5.14
), we derived an exact strong coupling expansion of the resummed conformal ladder integral and interpreted it as a sum over worldline instantons. It would be interesting to derive this expression from the first-quantized worldline formalism, generalizing the analysis in
[
10
]
.

6.2
Other important future directions

Less supersymmetric states.

In this paper, we analyzed the fluctuations around the
1/2
BPS large charge states. One interesting future direction is to generalize the analysis to less supersymmetric states, most notably to the
1/16
BPS states, which are dual to supersymmetric black holes in the large
N_{c}
limit. Concrete questions in this direction are to perform the semiclassical analysis around the
1/16
BPS solutions analyzed in
[
131
,
132
]
and to understand the residual (centrally-extended) symmetry around the background.

In addition, in a recent paper
[
133
]
, the authors analyzed operators in generic (nonsupersymmetric) CFTs that have both large spin and large charge, and identified a new phase called the “giant vortex” phase. The
1/16
BPS states are interesting also in this regard since they can carry nonzero Lorentz spin unlike the
1/2
BPS states discussed in this paper, and might provide a supersymmetric analog of the giant vortex phase.

Higher-rank gauge groups.

Most of the analyses performed in this paper are for the
{\rm SU}(2)
gauge group and a natural next step is to generalize it to higher-rank gauge groups. In higher-rank theories, there are multiple
1/2
BPS operators with a given R-charge, and correspondingly, there are different semiclassical backgrounds depending on how one distributes the charge among the Cartan directions (see below for
SU(3)
);

\langle\phi\rangle_{\rm cl}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi^{0}_{11}&&\\
&\phi^{0}_{22}&\\
&&\phi^{0}_{33}\end{array}\right)\qquad\phi^{0}_{11}+\phi^{0}_{22}+\phi^{0}_{33}=0\,.

(6.1)

Even in these cases, the centrally-extended
\mathfrak{psu}(2|2)^{2}
still governs the spectrum but the values of the central charges for the magnons depend on how one distributes the charges (i.e.
\phi_{ii}^{0}-\phi_{jj}^{0}
). In particular, we expect that the charge distribution along the Cartan to become continuous in the large
N_{c}
limit and the magnons can take an arbitrary continuous value of the central charge. This would be a potential way to relate the analysis in this paper to the centrally-extended symmetry in the planar limit
[
45
,
46
]
.

Three-point functions.

In Section
5.2
, we computed the structure constant of the Konishi operator and two large charge operators. A possible generalization would be to extend the analysis to other non-BPS operators. Even more interesting would be to consider the structure constants of three heavy non-BPS operators which are obtained by small deformation of large charge
1/2
BPS operators.
53
53

53

The paper
[
134
]
analyzed the correlation function of three large charge operators using the semi-classics. It might be possible to perform a similar analysis for
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. However for the non-extremal three-point function, it seems difficult to even find a semiclassical saddle point at large charge. (The configuration discussed in
[
134
]
corresponds to the extremal correlator in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM.)
In the planar limit, it was shown in
[
48
]
that the (non-extremal) three-point function of
1/2
BPS operators preserves a single copy of the centrally-extended
\mathfrak{psu}(2|2)
and one can use it to constrain the structure constants of non-BPS operators. The same symmetry should survive also in the large charge ’t Hooft limit, and it would be interesting to understand the constraints thereof.

Large spin ’t Hooft limit.

Yet another direction is to see if there exists an analog of the large charge ’t Hooft limit for the Lorentz spin
S
. Since the anomalous dimension of large spin operators scales as
g_{\rm YM}^{2}\log S
at weak coupling, one candidate limit is to send
S
to infinity keeping
g_{\rm YM}^{2}\log S
fixed. To understand the physics of this limit, it is useful to employ the coordinate transformation used in
[
135
]
and map a large spin operator to a state in
{\rm AdS}_{3}\times{\rm S}^{1}
. As discussed in Section 2.2 of
[
135
]
, the large spin operator corresponds to a fluxtube state in this geometry even at finite
N_{c}
as long as the coupling
g_{\rm YM}
is small,
54
54

54

This is because the probability of the fluxtube to break is exponentially suppressed
e^{-1/g_{\rm YM}^{2}}
in the limit.
and one can study it by (Kaluza-Klein) reducing
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM to an effective two-dimensional Yang-Mills with higher derivative corrections. It would be interesting to compute the anomalous dimensions in this limit using the perturbative dilatation operator and compare them against the effective
2d
YM description.

Relatedly, there have been interesting works
[
136
,
137
]
which identified a double-scaled lightlike limit of correlation functions at large
N_{c}
, where the ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda
is sent to zero with the “cusp times”
t_{i}^{2}=\lambda\log x_{i-1,i}^{2}\log x_{i,i+1}^{2}
held fixed. Physically this limit is controlled by operators with large spin and is similar to the large spin limit discussed above. To make a more concrete connection, it would be interesting to study a similar double-scaling limit (with
\lambda
replaced with
g_{\rm YM}^{2}
) for the correlation functions in the
{\rm SU}(2)
theory.

In addition, the results in
[
137
]
are given by determinants of Bessel functions, which also show up in the analysis of the large
N_{c}
2d YM. One interesting question is if the correlation functions studied in
[
137
]
can be mapped to some observables
55
55

55

The correlation function in the light-like limit is known to be related to a null polygonal Wilson loop
[
138
]
. In the planar limit, the null polygonal Wilson loop at finite
\lambda
can be studied using the so-called Pentagon decomposition
[
139
]
, in which the basic building block is the pentagon form factor which can be interpreted as a form factor of a branch-point twist operator
[
140
]
on the two-dimensional worldsheet. It is therefore tempting to conjecture that the relevant observable in this limit is a branch-point twist operator in
2d
YM.
in the effective 2d YM.

Combining large
N
and large
J
and black holes.

For the application to (standard) holography, it is important to study the combined large
N
-large
J
limits. One difficulty in this case is that the semiclassical analysis cannot be easily justified. To understand this point, let us recall the basics of the standard ’t Hooft limit. The action of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM is proportional to
N/\lambda
, which is infinitely large in the ’t Hooft limit,

S\sim\frac{N}{\lambda}\int d^{4}x{\rm Tr}\left[\cdots\right]\,.

(6.2)

One might therefore think that the limit can be studied using the saddle point approximation. As is well-known, this conclusion is incorrect for the following reason: if we approximate the path integral by its saddle point, the saddle point action scales like

S_{\rm saddle}\sim\frac{N}{\lambda}\left.{\rm Tr}\left[\cdots\right]\right|_{\rm saddle}\sim\frac{N^{2}}{\lambda}\,.

(6.3)

However, since there are
\sim N^{2}
dynamical degrees of freedom, the one-loop correction to the saddle point contribution also scales like

S_{\text{1-loop}}\sim N^{2}\,.

(6.4)

Thus, unless we take
\lambda
to be small, the saddle-point approximation cannot be justified.

One potential way to overcome this difficulty is to combine it with the large
J
limit. For instance, when the saddle point configuration scales like
56
56

56

The scaling with respect to
g_{\rm YM}
and
J
is fixed by the fact that the Noether charge is quadratic in fields and proportional to
g_{\rm YM}^{2}
; namely
J\sim\frac{1}{g_{\rm YM}^{2}}{\rm Tr}[\Phi_{\rm cl}^{2}]
. The exponent
\alpha
depends on how the charge is distributed among different components of the
{\rm SU}(N)
adjoint field. For instance, if the semiclassical configuration only has a single non-vanishing diagonal entry, we expect
\alpha=0
while if it is distributed equally among
N
diagonal entries, we expect
\alpha=1
.

g_{\rm YM}\sqrt{J}/N^{\alpha}
, (
6.3
) will be enhanced to

S_{\rm saddle}\sim\frac{N^{2}}{\lambda}\frac{g_{\rm YM}^{2}J}{N^{2\alpha}}\,,

(6.5)

while the scaling for
S_{\text{1-loop}}
remains the same. By taking the ratio
g_{\rm YM}\sqrt{J}/N^{\alpha}\sim\sqrt{\lambda J/N}/N^{\alpha}
to be large, we can then suppress the 1-loop correction parametrically. Of course the estimate we gave here is rather crude and it is important to perform detailed analysis for concrete cases.

It would be interesting to study double-scaling limits also on the gravity side. When the ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda
is infinite, the bulk is described by supergravity. As we decrease
\lambda
, it receives higher derivative corrections proportional to some powers of
1/\lambda
. On the other hand, the effects of these higher derivative corrections are suppressed for a black hole of a very large size. Since the size of the black hole with a large quantum number
J
is controlled roughly by the parameter
J/N^{2}
, the effective strength of higher derivative corrections is parametrized by
\frac{N^{2}}{\lambda^{\beta}J}
with some positive exponent
\beta
. This offers the possibility to study the limit
\frac{N^{2}}{\lambda^{\beta}J}\ll 1
both at weak and strong couplings. At strong coupling, it is described by supergravity as discussed above, while we can try to reach this limit from the weak coupling by first taking the double-scaling limit in which
\lambda\to 0
with
\tilde{\lambda}\equiv\lambda^{\beta}J/N^{2}
fixed and then later taking the limit
\tilde{\lambda}\to\infty
, much like what we did in this paper for the strong coupling limit of
\lambda_{J}
. Note that there is no guarantee that different ways of taking the limits give the same answer and more detailed analysis is certainly necessary.

\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT.

Much of our analysis can be generalized to study large charge operators and their correlation functions in the double-scaling limit for general
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SCFTs with a conformal manifold such as the superconformal QCD (SQCD) described by
{\mathcal{N}}=2

{\rm SU}(N)
SYM coupled to
2N
fundamental hypermultiplets. Previous works on large charge limits of these
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories have focused on the two-point function of BPS operators
[
23
,
27
,
26
]
which contain information about the extremal correlator sector of the SCFT
[
28
]
and are nontrivial due to the reduced supersymmetry. In particular, at strong coupling
\lambda_{J}\gg 1
, these two-point functions exhibit an infinite tower of worldline instanton contributions similar to what we have seen here for more general observables in the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
SYM.
Nonetheless, it would be much more interesting to extend the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
large charge analysis to non-BPS operators and also higher-point functions by
exploiting our methods here.

Indeed, one copy of the maximally-centrally-extended
\mathfrak{psu}(2|2)
symmetry that played an important role in our “bootstrap” analysis of the non-BPS large charge operator spectrum survives in general
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories. In this case, the superconformal R-symmetry is
{\mathfrak{u}}(1)_{R}\times\mathfrak{su}(2)_{R}
and the relevant large charge operators are Coulomb branch chiral primary operators
{\mathcal{O}}_{J}
that carry charge
J>0
under the
{\mathfrak{u}}(1)_{R}
and are singlet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{R}
. The BPS condition reads
\Delta=J
and thus as before, the operator inserted at origin is preserved by the superconformal generator
C\equiv{\hat{D}-\hat{J}\over 2}
. Together with the rotation symmetry
\mathfrak{so}(4)
and the
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{R}
symmetry, the full superconformal subalgebra preserved by the operator insertion
{\mathcal{O}}_{J}(0)
is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{su}(2)\times\mathfrak{psu}(2|2)\ltimes{\mathbb{R}}\,,

(6.6)

compared to (
3.4
) for the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
case (see around (
3.3
)). In the large charge ’t Hooft limit, we expect this symmetry to be further centrally extended, which includes a maximally-centrally-extended
\mathfrak{psu}(2|2)\ltimes{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
factor, that would govern the physics of excitations on the
large charge state created by
{\mathcal{O}}_{J}(0)
. It would be interesting to carry out this analysis explicitly, for example in the case of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2

{\rm SU}(2)
SQCD, to learn about the non-BPS large charge operators.

Another potentially intriguing direction is to develop a better understanding of the large charge EFT in the large charge ’t Hooft limit which shares many similarities with the conventional Coulomb branch EFT, as we have commented on in the main text, in general
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories.
It is well-known that the latter EFT has an elegant low energy description in terms of the Seiberg-Witten curve and the complex geometric data therein, largely thanks to the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Poincaré supersymmetry
[
122
,
123
]
. It is thus tantalizing to speculate a version of the Seiberg-Witten curve for the large charge EFT that follows from the centrally extended
\mathfrak{psu}(2|2)
symmetry.
57
57

57

See Section
3.1.4
for comments on the relation between the centrally extended
\mathfrak{psu}(2|2)
symmetry preserved in the large charge EFT and the Poincaré supersymmetry that govern the Coulomb branch EFT.
In particular, it would be interesting to extract the correlation functions of large charge Coulomb branch operators from such an EFT. We leave this investigation to future work.

Appendix A
Action & conventions

In this appendix, we establish our conventions for the flat space action, which will be used for perturbative computations. We begin with the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM action which we write as

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{N}=4{\,\rm{SYM}}}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x{\rm Tr\,}\left(-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}D_{\mu}\Phi_{I}D^{\mu}\Phi_{I}+\frac{1}{4}[\Phi_{I},\Phi_{J}][\Phi_{I},\Phi_{J}]+{\rm{fermions}}\right)\,,

(A.1)

where we omit the fermionic part of the action since we will not use it explicitly.

We express the six real scalar fields in terms of complex scalar fields in the usual manner,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{Z+\bar{Z}}{\sqrt{2}},\quad\Phi_{2}=\frac{Z-\bar{Z}}{i\sqrt{2}},\quad\Phi_{3}=\frac{X+\bar{X}}{\sqrt{2}},\quad\Phi_{4}=\frac{X-\bar{X}}{i\sqrt{2}},\quad\Phi_{5}=\frac{Y+\bar{Y}}{\sqrt{2}},\quad\Phi_{6}=\frac{Y-\bar{Y}}{i\sqrt{2}}.

(A.2)

Note that each of the complex scalar fields takes values on the Lie algebra of the gauge group SU(2), with the generators of the Lie algebra taken to be Hermitian in our conventions. This allows us to represent the fields explicitly as follows

\displaystyle Z=\begin{pmatrix}z^{0}&z^{+}\\
z^{-}&-z^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\bar{Z}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{z}^{0}&\bar{z}^{-}\\
\bar{z}^{+}&-\bar{z}^{0}\end{pmatrix},

(A.3)

\displaystyle X=\begin{pmatrix}x^{0}&x^{+}\\
x^{-}&-x^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\bar{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{x}^{0}&\bar{x}^{-}\\
\bar{x}^{+}&-\bar{x}^{0}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle Y=\begin{pmatrix}y^{0}&y^{+}\\
y^{-}&-y^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\bar{Y}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{y}^{0}&\bar{y}^{-}\\
\bar{y}^{+}&-\bar{y}^{0}\end{pmatrix}\,.

Similarly, the gluons, fermions, and ghost fields can be decomposed in the same way

A_{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{{}_{\mu}}^{0}&a_{{}_{\mu}}^{+}\\
a_{{}_{\mu}}^{-}&-a_{{}_{\mu}}^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\psi=\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{0}&\psi^{+}\\
\psi^{-}&-\psi^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bar{\psi}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}^{0}&\bar{\psi}^{-}\\
\bar{\psi}^{+}&-\bar{\psi}^{0}\end{pmatrix},

(A.4)

c=\begin{pmatrix}c^{0}&c^{+}\\
c^{-}&-c^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bar{c}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{c}^{0}&\bar{c}^{-}\\
\bar{c}^{+}&-\bar{c}^{0}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(A.5)

Among these fields,
Z
and
\bar{Z}
acquire a classical profile in the large charge background

Z_{\rm{cl}}=\begin{pmatrix}z_{{\rm{cl}}}^{0}&0\\
0&-z_{{\rm{cl}}}^{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bar{Z}_{\rm{cl}}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{z}_{{\rm{cl}}}^{0}&0\\
0&-\bar{z}_{{\rm{cl}}}^{0}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.6)

around which we expand the action. In order to perform perturbative computations, we first gauge fix the action by adding to it the gauge fixing term

\mathcal{S}_{\rm gf}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x\,\left(-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Tr\,}\left(\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}+i[Z,\bar{Z}_{\rm cl}]+i[\bar{Z},Z_{\rm cl}]\right)^{2}\right)

(A.7)

and the corresponding ghost term

\mathcal{S}_{\rm gh}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x\,{\rm Tr\,}\left(\bar{c}\left(\partial^{\mu}D_{\mu}c-[[c,Z],Z_{\rm cl}]-[[c,\bar{Z}],Z_{\rm cl}]-[[c,\bar{Z}_{\rm cl}],Z_{\rm cl}]-[[c,Z_{\rm cl}],\bar{Z}_{\rm cl}]\right)\right)\,,

(A.8)

where
c,\bar{c}
are the ghost fields.
The resulting effective action can be split as follows

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{N}=4{\,\rm{SYM}}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm gf}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm gh}=\mathcal{S}_{\rm{kin}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{m,b}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic}}+{\rm{fermions}}\,,

(A.9)

where from now on we will omit completely the discussion about the fermionic part.
The kinetic terms piece
\mathcal{S}_{\rm{kin}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm{kin}}=\frac{2}{g_{\rm{YM}}^{2}}\int d^{4}x\Bigl[a_{\mu}^{0}\Box(a^{0})^{\mu}+a_{\mu}^{+}\Box(a^{-})^{\mu}+2\sum_{\Phi\in\{x,y,z,c\}}\bar{\Phi}^{0}\Box\Phi^{0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Phi\in\{x,y,z,c\}\\
p=\pm\end{subarray}}\bar{\Phi}^{p}\Box\Phi^{p}\Bigr].

(A.10)

where

\Box=\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}
.
The bosonic mass part of the action
\mathcal{S}_{\rm{m,b}}
reads

\mathcal{S}_{\rm{m,b}}=-\frac{16}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x\Bigl[|z^{0}_{\rm{cl}}|^{2}\Bigl(a^{+}_{\mu}(a^{-})^{\mu}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Phi\in\{x,y,z\}\\
p=\pm\end{subarray}}|\Phi^{p}|^{2}\bigr)+\frac{i}{2}\,\partial^{\mu}\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}\bigl(z^{+}a^{-}_{\mu}-z^{-}a^{+}_{\mu}\bigr)+\frac{i}{2}\,\partial^{\mu}{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}\bigl(\bar{z}^{-}a^{-}_{\mu}-\bar{z}^{+}a^{+}_{\mu}\bigr)\Bigr].

(A.11)

We note that the classical profile of
z^{0}_{\rm{cl}}
and
\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}
induces a mixing term between the gauge fields and the scalars
z^{\pm},\bar{z}^{\pm}
.

We now proceed with the cubic part of the action, which due to its length can be sub-split into several parts according to the field content

\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic}}=\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic,g}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic,s}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic,g,s}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic,s,gh}}\,.

(A.12)

Each one of these parts read as

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{cubic,g}}=\frac{2}{g_{\mathrm{YM}}^{2}}\int d^{4}x\,2i\Big[(a_{\mu}^{-}a_{\nu}^{0}-a_{\nu}^{-}a_{\mu}^{0})\partial^{\nu}(a^{\mu})^{+}+(a_{\nu}^{+}a_{\mu}^{0}-a_{\mu}^{+}a_{\nu}^{0})\partial^{\nu}(a^{\mu})^{-}+(a_{\mu}^{+}a_{\nu}^{-}-a_{\mu}^{-}a_{\nu}^{+})\partial^{\nu}(a^{0})^{\mu}\Big]\,,

(A.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic,s}}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x

\displaystyle 4\Biggl[\sum_{\Phi\in\{x,y\}}\Bigl(\bar{\Phi}^{0}\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}(\Phi^{-}z^{+}+\Phi^{+}z^{-})+\bar{\Phi}^{0}z^{0}_{\rm{cl}}(\Phi^{-}\bar{z}^{-}+\Phi^{+}\bar{z}^{+})-2\,\bar{z}^{0}z_{\rm{cl}}^{0}(|\Phi^{+}|^{2}+|\Phi^{-}|^{2})\Bigr)

(A.14)

\displaystyle-\,\bigl(|z^{-}|^{2}+|z^{+}|^{2}\bigr)\bar{z}^{0}z_{\rm{cl}}^{0}+2\,z^{-}z^{+}\bar{z}^{0}\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}+h.c.\Biggr]\,,

where
h.c.
means adding the conjugate of all terms of the Lagrangian where unbarred fields become barred and vice-versa. The remaining cubic terms read

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\text{cubic,g,s}}=\frac{4}{g_{\text{YM}}^{2}}\int d^{4}x

\displaystyle\Biggl[-2(a^{-})^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{0}(z^{+}\bar{z}_{\text{cl}}^{0}+\bar{z}^{-}z_{\text{cl}}^{0})-2(a^{+})^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{0}(z^{-}\bar{z}_{\text{cl}}^{0}+\bar{z}^{+}z_{\text{cl}}^{0})

(A.15)

\displaystyle-4(a^{-})^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{+}(\bar{z}^{0}z_{\text{cl}}^{0}+z^{0}\bar{z}_{\text{cl}}^{0})+\sum_{\Phi\in\{x,y,z\}}\biggl(i\partial^{\mu}\Phi^{-}(\bar{\Phi}^{-}a_{\mu}^{0}-\bar{\Phi}^{0}a_{\mu}^{+})

\displaystyle+i\partial^{\mu}\Phi^{+}(\bar{\Phi}^{0}a_{\mu}^{-}-\bar{\Phi}^{+}a_{\mu}^{0})+i\partial^{\mu}\Phi^{0}(\bar{\Phi}^{+}a_{\mu}^{+}-\bar{\Phi}^{-}a_{\mu}^{-})+h.c.\biggr)\Biggr]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm{cubic,s,gh}}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x

\displaystyle\Biggl[\bar{z}^{0}z^{0}_{\rm{cl}}\left(2\bar{c}^{-}c^{-}+2\bar{c}^{+}c^{+}\right)+z^{0}\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}\left(2\bar{c}^{-}c^{-}+2\bar{c}^{+}c^{+}\right)

\displaystyle-\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}\left(2z^{-}\bar{c}^{-}+2z^{+}\bar{c}^{+}\right)c^{0}-z^{0}_{\rm{cl}}\left(2\bar{c}^{+}\bar{z}^{-}+2\bar{c}^{-}\bar{z}^{+}\right)c^{0}\Biggr]\,.

Finally, we display explicitly the quartic part of the action which we write as

\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic}}=\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,g}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,g,s}}+\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,s}}

(A.16)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,g}}=\frac{1}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x

\displaystyle\Biggl[4(a^{0})^{\mu}(a^{0})^{\nu}\left(a^{+}_{\mu}a^{-}_{\nu}+a^{-}_{\mu}a^{+}_{\nu}\right)-2\,a^{-}_{\nu}(a^{+})^{\nu}\left(a^{-}_{\mu}(a^{+})^{\mu}+2a^{0}_{\mu}(a^{0})^{\mu}\right)

(A.17)

\displaystyle-4\,a^{-}_{\mu}(a^{+})^{\mu}a^{0}_{\nu}(a^{0})^{\nu}+a^{+}_{\mu}\left(a^{+}\right)^{\mu}\left(a^{-}\right)^{\nu}a^{-}_{\nu}+a^{-}_{\mu}(a^{-})^{\mu}a^{+}_{\nu}(a^{+})^{\nu}\Biggr]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,g,s}}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\sum_{\Phi\in\{x,y,z\}}\int d^{4}x

\displaystyle\Biggl[\,4\,a^{-}_{\mu}(a^{0})^{\mu}\left(\Phi^{+}\bar{\Phi}^{0}+\bar{\Phi}^{-}\Phi^{0}\right)+2\,a^{-}_{\mu}\left(a^{-}\right)^{\mu}\left(\Phi^{+}\bar{\Phi}^{-}\right)

(A.18)

\displaystyle+4\,a^{+}_{\mu}(a^{0})^{\mu}\left(\Phi^{-}\bar{\Phi}^{0}+\Phi^{0}\bar{\Phi}^{+}\right)+2\,a^{+}_{\mu}\left(a^{+}\right)^{\mu}\left(\Phi^{-}\bar{\Phi}^{+}\right)

\displaystyle-2\,(a^{-}_{\mu}(a^{+})^{\mu}+2a^{0}_{\mu}(a^{0})^{\mu})\left(\Phi^{-}\bar{\Phi}^{-}+\Phi^{+}\bar{\Phi}^{+}\right)-8\,a^{-}_{\mu}(a^{+})^{\mu}\left(\Phi^{0}\bar{\Phi}^{0}\right)\Biggr]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,s}}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Phi\in\{x,y,z\}\\
\Psi\in\{x,y,z\}\end{subarray}}\Biggl[-4\bar{\Psi}^{-}\bar{\Phi}^{+}\Psi^{0}\Phi^{0}+4\Psi^{-}\bar{\Phi}^{-}\Phi^{0}\bar{\Psi}^{0}-8\Psi^{-}\bar{\Psi}^{-}\Phi^{0}\bar{\Phi}^{0}

\displaystyle+4\Psi^{+}\bar{\Phi}^{+}\Phi^{0}\bar{\Psi}^{0}-8\Psi^{+}\bar{\Psi}^{+}\Phi^{0}\bar{\Phi}^{0}-\Psi^{-}\Phi^{-}\bar{\Psi}^{-}\bar{\Phi}^{-}-\Psi^{+}\Phi^{+}\bar{\Psi}^{+}\bar{\Phi}^{+}

\displaystyle-2\Psi^{+}\Phi^{-}\bar{\Psi}^{+}\bar{\Phi}^{-}+4\Psi^{-}\Phi^{+}\bar{\Psi}^{0}\bar{\Phi}^{0}+4\Psi^{+}\Phi^{-}\bar{\Psi}^{-}\bar{\Phi}^{+}\Biggr]\,.

Appendix B
Large charge background and scalar propagators

In this section, we aim to find the propagators for the scalar fields in the effective action. We consider a two point function of local operators with a very large charge, which we denote as

O_{J}(x)=(z^{0}(x))^{J}\,,\quad\bar{O}_{J}(x)=(\bar{z}^{0}(x))^{J}\,.

(B.1)

In the limit when
J\rightarrow\infty
, the insertion of such operators in the path integral source a non-trivial profile for
z^{0}
and
\bar{z}^{0}
which can be obtained as a saddle point of the two-point function given by

\langle\bar{O}_{J}(x_{1})O_{J}(x_{2})\rangle\simeq e^{S_{\rm{eff}}^{\ast}}\quad{\rm{as}}\quad J\rightarrow\infty\;,\;g_{\rm{YM}}\rightarrow 0\quad{\rm{with}}\quad\lambda_{J}=g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}J/2\;{\rm{fixed}}\,,

(B.2)

where the corresponding effective action is given by

S_{\rm{eff}}^{\ast}=S_{\rm{eff}}\Big\rvert_{z^{0},\,\bar{z}^{0}\rightarrow z^{0}_{\rm{cl}},\,\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}}\;\;{\rm with}\;\;S_{\rm{eff}}=\frac{2J}{\lambda_{J}}\int d^{4}x\,\left[\bar{z}^{0}\Box z^{0}+\frac{\lambda_{J}}{2}\left(\log(\bar{z}^{0})\,\delta(x-x_{1})+\log(z^{0})\,\delta(x-x_{2})\right)\right]\,.

(B.3)

In this expression,
z^{0}_{\rm{cl}},\,\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}
are the solutions of the saddle point equations that follow from the action
S_{\rm{eff}}
.
The explicit solutions for
z^{0}_{\rm{cl}}(x)
and
\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}(x)
are given by

z^{0}_{\rm{cl}}(x)=\frac{e^{i\phi}\,|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{2\pi|x-x_{1}|^{2}}\,\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{J}}{2}}\,,\quad\bar{z}^{0}_{\rm{cl}}(x)=\frac{e^{-i\phi}\,|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{2\pi|x-x_{2}|^{2}}\,\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{J}}{2}}\,.

(B.4)

From (
A.11
), we find that the scalars
z^{\pm},x^{\pm}
and
y^{\pm}
have masses given by

m^{2}(x)=8|z_{\rm{cl}}^{0}|^{2}=\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}{\pi^{2}|x-x_{1}|^{2}|x-x_{2}|^{2}}\,\lambda_{J}\,.

(B.5)

The quadratic part of the action for each of these scalars, which we generically denote by
\Phi
, is then given by

\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quad},\Phi}=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\int d^{4}x\left(\bar{\Phi}\Box\Phi-m^{2}(x)|\Phi|^{2}\right)\,.

(B.6)

The scalar propagator is obtained by solving the equation

\quad(-\Box_{x}+m^{2}(x))\,G(x,y)=\,\delta^{(4)}(x-y)\qquad{\rm{with}}\quad\,G(x,y):=\frac{2}{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}\langle\bar{\Phi}(x)\Phi(y)\rangle\,.

(B.7)

The solution to this equation has already been worked out in
[
9
]
. One considers an expansion in powers the ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda_{J}

G(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}g_{k}(x,y)\,,\quad g_{k}(x,y)\sim\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{J}^{k})\,.

(B.8)

The leading order term satisfies the massless propagator equation

-\Box_{x}g_{0}=\delta^{(4)}(x-y)\quad\Rightarrow\quad g_{0}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}\,,

(B.9)

whereas the remaining terms obey

-\Box_{x}g_{k+1}+m^{2}g_{k}=0\,,

(B.10)

where we have omitted the arguments for simplicity. It is simple to obtain iteratively the perturbative solution at any order
k

g_{k}(x,y)=(-1)^{k}\left(\prod_{n=1}^{k}\int d^{4}z_{n}\,m^{2}(z_{n})g_{0}(z_{n},z_{n+1})\right)g_{0}(x,z_{1})\,,

(B.11)

where we defined
z_{k+1}\equiv y
. More explicitly, this is equivalent to

g_{k}(x,y)=\left(-4g^{2}\right)^{k}\frac{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}}{4\pi^{2}(x-x_{1})^{2}(y-x_{2})^{2}}\,F^{(k)}(z,\bar{z})

(B.12)

where
F^{(k)}(z,\bar{z})
stems from a ladder type integral and can be pictorially represented by:

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{ \includegraphics[scale]{ladder}}}=\frac{\pi^{2k}}{(y-x)^{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2k}}(1-z)(1-\bar{z})F^{(k)}(z,\bar{z})

Here the integration points are represented by the black dots while the white dots are unintegrated external points. The conformal cross-ratios
z,\bar{z}
are given by

z\bar{z}\equiv{(y-x_{1})^{2}(x-x_{2})^{2}\over(x-x_{1})^{2}(y-x_{2})^{2}}\,,\quad(1-z)(1-\bar{z})\equiv{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}(x-y)^{2}\over(x-x_{1})^{2}(y-x_{2})^{2}}\,.

(B.13)

With the explicit form of the ladder integrals
[
141
,
142
]
, it was found in
[
47
]
the resumed expression giving the two-point function (
B.8
)

\langle\bar{\Phi}(x)\Phi(y)\rangle=\frac{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}{4\pi^{2}|x-y|^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\frac{\sqrt{\frac{z\bar{z}}{(1-z)^{2}(1-\bar{z})^{2}}}\sinh(t-\frac{1}{2}\log{z\bar{z}})\,J_{0}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{J}}{\pi^{2}}\,t\,(t-\log z\bar{z})}\right)}{\left(1-\frac{1+z\bar{z}}{(1-z)(1-\bar{z})}+2\sqrt{\frac{z\bar{z}}{(1-z)^{2}(1-\bar{z})^{2}}}\cosh\left(t-\frac{1}{2}\log{z\bar{z}}\right)\right)^{2}}

(B.14)

where
J_{k}
denotes the Bessel function.
The ladder integrals can be represented in an alternative form that is more useful for analyzing the strong coupling behavior of correlation functions
[
47
,
94
,
49
]
. It is given by

\langle\bar{\Phi}(x)\Phi(y)\rangle=\frac{g^{2}_{\rm{YM}}}{8\pi^{2}(x-y)^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}(-4g^{2})^{n}\frac{(1-z)(1-\bar{z})}{z-\bar{z}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{du}{2\pi}\,\frac{2a\sin(a\varphi)e^{-2iu\sigma}}{\left(u^{2}+\frac{a^{2}}{4}\right)^{n+1}}

(B.15)

where
e^{i\varphi}=\sqrt{z/\bar{z}}
and
e^{-\sigma}=\sqrt{z\bar{z}}
. Since this type of integrals are common in integrability approaches to correlation functions
[
49
]
, we will refer to it as the
integrability-like
representation of the ladder integral.

Of particular importance are the following limits of this formula. When
\delta^{2}:=|x-x_{1}|^{2}\rightarrow 0
we obtain
[
9
]

\langle\bar{\Phi}(x_{1})\Phi(y)\rangle=\frac{4g^{2}}{J}\frac{1}{|x_{1}-y|^{2}\sqrt{1+16g^{2}}}\left(\frac{\delta|x_{2}-y|}{|x_{1}-x_{2}||x_{1}-y|}\right)^{\sqrt{1+16g^{2}}-1}

(B.16)

and when
|y-x_{2}|^{2}\rightarrow 0

\langle\bar{\Phi}(x)\Phi(x_{2})\rangle=\frac{4g^{2}}{J}\frac{1}{|x_{2}-x|^{2}\sqrt{1+16g^{2}}}\left(\frac{\delta|x_{1}-x|}{|x_{1}-x_{2}||x_{2}-x|}\right)^{\sqrt{1+16g^{2}}-1}\,.

(B.17)

Appendix C
Perturbative computation

In this appendix, we set up the perturbative computation of one-loop diagrams from the effective action derived in the Appendix
A
around the background field determined in Appendix
B
. We will be computing the diagrams listed below:

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.88}{$G_{1}:=$}\vbox{\hbox{ \includegraphics[scale]{4scalarvertex}}}\quad\quad\scalebox{0.88}{$G_{2}:=$}\vbox{\hbox{ \includegraphics[scale]{4scalarvertex2}}}\quad\quad\scalebox{0.88}{$G^{\pm}_{3}:=$}\vbox{\hbox{ \includegraphics[scale]{gluonexchange}}}\,.

The double line corresponds to the massive scalar propagator and the coiled line represents the propagator of the massless mode of the gluon field.

Diagram
G_{1}
.

After factoring out the tree level contribution and using the part of the action denoted by
\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,s}}
in (
A.18
), we obtain the following one-loop result:

G_{1}=-\frac{32g^{2}}{J}\frac{1}{1+16g^{2}}\,\pi^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\,|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{4}X_{1122}\,,

(C.1)

which is independent of the sign of
y
. We consider the simultaneous point-splitting
x_{3}^{\mu}=x_{1}^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}
and
x_{4}^{\mu}=x_{2}^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}
with
\epsilon^{\mu}\rightarrow 0
of the integral
X_{1234}
given by

X_{1234}=\int\frac{d^{4}z}{(2\pi)^{8}}\frac{1}{|x_{1}-z|^{2}|x_{2}-z|^{2}|x_{3}-z|^{2}|x_{4}-z|^{2}},.

(C.2)

The result is given by
[
98
]

X_{1122}\equiv\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{3}\rightarrow x_{1}\\
x_{4}\rightarrow x_{2}\end{subarray}}X_{1234}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\left(1-\log\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}\right)\right)\times\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{4}}.

(C.3)

We conclude that the logarithmic divergence of such a diagram is

G_{1}\big\rvert_{\log}=\frac{1}{J}\frac{4g^{2}}{1+16g^{2}}\simeq\frac{1}{J}\left(4g^{2}-64g^{4}+\dots\right).

(C.4)

Diagram
G_{2}
.

The diagram
G_{2}
is similar to
G_{1}
except with a different combinatorial factor. We get

G_{2}=\frac{64g^{2}}{J}\frac{1}{1+16g^{2}}\,\pi^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\,|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{4}X_{1122}\,.

(C.5)

This gives the following logarithmic divergence:

G_{2}\big\rvert_{\log}=-\frac{1}{J}\frac{8g^{2}}{1+16g^{2}}\simeq\frac{1}{J}\left(-8g^{2}+128g^{4}+\dots\right).

(C.6)

Diagram
G_{3}^{\pm}
.

The gluon exchange diagram is derived from the part of the action denoted by
\mathcal{S}_{\rm{quartic,g,s}}
in (
A.18
) and produces the following result

G_{3}^{\pm}=\mp\frac{64g^{2}}{J}\frac{1}{1+16g^{2}}(2\pi)^{6}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{4}\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{3}\rightarrow x_{1}\\
x_{4}\rightarrow x_{2}\end{subarray}}\partial_{2}\cdot\partial_{4}H_{12,34}

(C.7)

with the two-fold integral
H_{12,34}
given by

H_{12,34}=\int\frac{d^{4}zd^{4}w}{(2\pi)^{10}}\frac{1}{|x_{1}-z|^{2}|x_{2}-z|^{2}|w-z|^{2}|x_{3}-w|^{2}|x_{4}-w|^{2}}\,.

(C.8)

We use the result for its derivatives that can be found for example in
[
98
]
and reads

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{3}\rightarrow x_{1}\\
x_{4}\rightarrow x_{2}\end{subarray}}\partial_{2}\cdot\partial_{4}H_{12,34}=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left(2-\log\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}\right)\right)\times\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{4}}\,.

(C.9)

When extracting the logarithmic divergence we now obtain

G_{3}^{\pm}\big\rvert_{\log}=\pm\frac{1}{J}\frac{16g^{2}}{1+16g^{2}}\simeq\pm\frac{1}{J}\left(16g^{2}-256g^{4}+\dots\right)\,.

(C.10)

This concludes the perturbative one-loop computation.
